## Appearance

In the old life he was a large man gone soft in the middle, with forearms like a butcher's and a left knee that clicked when the weather turned. His hands were short-fingered and steady, with a pale line where a wedding ring had been; his hair went early at the temples, and on post-call days he shaved badly and did not care. He smelled of hand sanitiser, machine coffee and the faint metallic tang of a hospital cleaned too many times with the same mop. People read his face as anger. It was fatigue, and he never corrected them, because correcting them cost something.

In the new life all of that is folded into an infant body that cannot hold up its own head. The surgeon's hands are now small soft wrists with milky nails; the butcher's forearms are gone; the clicking knee is a soft unformed joint that will click again in time. Across his ribs and down his forearm runs a branching red mark like a frost-flower or a burn map, which the midwife Alda Reed crossed herself at and called a birth-flower, and which is in truth the fire writing itself into the hot trace that crossed with him. He drools. He cannot turn over. He is washed, dressed and discussed in the third person by people who do not know there is a forty-year-old man listening. He is a trauma surgeon who cannot reach a cup. He counts the nails in the floorboards because there is nothing else to count, four on the near board, four on the next, and a fifth that is not a nail but a knot the width of a thumb.

## Voice & Manner

He speaks little, plainly, low, and exactly. His question is usually the shortest one in the room. He does not use the old-life idiom with people who would find it strange; he never says *I was a surgeon once*, because no words in Grath would carry it. With the few adults who can bear it, the Chicago rhythm comes back: faster, profane, warm under the withering. Under danger his sentences get shorter, not longer, and the clinical register takes over completely.

- “How long since he stopped moving? Not the story. How long.”
- “I'm not asking you to pray. Hand me the clamp.”
- “That's a lovely account. Shame nobody's holding the heat.”
- “Leave it. It's gone.” — the sentence that is his gift and his heresy in Sarn.
- “Don't set that down. You'll pay for it in the thaw.” — learned from the clay yard, said in the local proverb-form before he has fully learned to mean it.
- Remembered, in the old life: \memory{You're back on in ten, Marchetti.} said in a voice like a file.
- Ch 7: he has learned to find the joke funny without laughing --- a way of laughing that costs less and keeps the same blade in it. He keeps the count in loaves and glances, in the kiln's four, four and a gap, and in the space where a fifth thing should be, and tells nobody what it counts.

He cannot stop reading bodies. At a family dinner he noticed a new knuckle on his brother's hand and a cut his aunt wiped on her skirt before anyone saw. He counts without deciding to: pulses per ten seconds, exits in any room, steps from the bed to the door. His humour is fast and withering and used like a handrail. He finishes everything on his plate, including food he dislikes, because waste offends him in a way he cannot explain.

## History

He was born in 1984 and raised in a two-storey brick bungalow in Hermosa, on a block where the el ran high and iron over Pulaski and the summer smelled of charcoal and wet pavement. His father Aldo was a machinist from near Bari who showed love by fixing things and never learned to say it; his mother Theresa worked as a nurse's aide and came home smelling of bleach and lilac soap. There were three children: Dan, Jimmy the patrol officer, and Lisa who left for Florida. The house was loud and full and he learned from it volume, profanity, the duty of finishing everything on his plate, and the certainty that love is a practical act refused in conversation. He went to Lane Tech because his mother insisted, took things apart on the shop floor, and found the human body in the cadaver lab at the University of Illinois Chicago the way other boys find engines: by being forbidden and doing it anyway. Anatomy did not argue. It had a right answer under your hands.

He trained at Cook County, became an attending, worked nights because nights paid more and days were when the administration was awake. What he knew, precisely: airway in blood and dark, a cricothyrotomy with a knife and tube if the mouth was destroyed; the difference between pulsing blood and welling blood, packing a cavity, the third rib that tears the lung; reducing shoulders and pinning femurs; burns by the rule of nines and the Parkland formula; hypothermia the way a priest knows sin — the afterdrop, the fact that a cold patient cannot clot, that the grimace of severe cold looks like a smile, that you do not rub a frozen foot; sterile field, debridement, leaving a wound open; the small evidence a body carries; and above all triage, the ninety-second sorting of twenty injured people into those who will live, those who will live only if he chooses them, and those who will die whatever he does. He did it hundreds of times and never once found a way to make it feel clean.

What he did not know mattered as much. He was a clinician, not a physicist. He could not grow penicillin, anaesthetise with a weed, make a fire with his hands, track a beast, bargain for food, read a Sarnish ledger, or make a native believe a clamped vessel might survive. He was an amateur boxer from his twenties — enough to keep his chin down and take one punch and wait. He knew exactly how a body fails and was almost helpless when the body in question was his own.

He married Elena Rossi in 2007; their daughter Nina was born in 2000. The shifts ate the marriage. He was absent for the dinners and the small ordinary evenings that turn out to be the whole of a life. The divorce was quiet and worse for it. He kept a photograph of Nina at six on his wall and looked at it every night without seeing it. There was Marisol Vega after, an emergency-room nurse kind enough to try for eleven months, who left because she was tired of being the woman who waits. There was Des Carter, who made jokes instead of asking; Ostrowski, who taught him to decide and carry; Vesci, who called him the butcher and never forgave the report.

When he was thirty-one a twenty-two-year-old named Tasha Broussard came in after a fight, was examined, had a normal blood pressure and a bruised abdomen, and was discharged with a sprain. She returned four hours later in cardiac arrest from a ruptured spleen the size of a fist. The junior had missed the sign; Dan, covering the shift, signed the chart without reading the nurse's note that said she had been vomiting. At the review he let the hospital call it a systems failure and said nothing about the note. He kept his job, his licence and his reputation, and carried the shape of his own conduct for the rest of his life. His father died that spring while Dan was on a thirty-six-hour shift; he noticed the casket was slightly crooked in the aisle and thought about it for years.

He died in February, in a blizzard, on the expressway at 2:17 in the morning, going back to the minivan for the last time and getting the child out before the fire took the van and took him. His phone was lit in his coat with a name he would not look at. The last thing he knew, orderless: the hiss of fuel, the orange light on snow, the cold leaving his hands, and a wordless sentence that was nevertheless complete — *I have left the job unfinished.* A woman in a coat the colour of wet ash stood at the edge of the flare light writing on a slate, and her writing did not stop.

He woke as an infant in Low Kiln, taken in by Sella Vail of the kiln-house, with the birth-flower across his ribs and a warm stone at his belly that was already going cold.

## Wounds & Fears

The body's humiliations are exact: drool, the useless head, the blanket that will not stay on, the small animal sound that comes when he turns his head past a point, the cup two paces off that he cannot reach. Day 1 is lived awake from the birth to morning; nobody thinks to ask whether he wants to be awake. By the afternoon of Day 3, under the Vail roof, heat has come back into his hands and feet with the itch and sting of a rewarming, which he reads in reverse; the pale stone that held the last of that warmth was left in the fold of grey wool in House Silt, and he feels the place of it as an empty place felt with an exactness that has nothing to do with temperature. But the deeper wounds are old ones. The missed call to Nina, promising a boat on Lake Michigan for nine years. Tasha's chart and the note he did not read. The crooked casket nobody corrected. A sister in Florida, a mother's kitchen, a father who could not say what he felt and so fixed things.

He fears being the only adult in the room and him with the hands of a child. He fears that his clinical gift is not a gift at all but a moral defect — that the willingness to say *leave it* says something about him he would rather not know. He fears the hollow and the Kept: a vessel that holds perfectly and feels nothing, which is the thing he has spent his life doing to himself in the name of duty. He fears going home most of all, because the mechanism is real and the price is the warmth of the woman the child in the minivan became. He cannot pay that account by dying again; it can only be carried.

His contradictions are the engine of him. He who decided who was beyond saving now lives in a world whose founding doctrine is that nobody is ever beyond keeping. He who fled intimacy now carries a whole district's debt. He distrusts institutions because he has performed their gesture himself — and yet he learns their trades, joins a household, and will one day wear the Court's own disciplines like a skin. He is most himself when he is least comfortable, which is a dangerous thing to be in a world where comfort is stolen from the poor and he will not notice that somebody else is paying for his.

## Desires

He wants to go home. He wants to hear Nina's voice, to say the nine-year-old sentence about the lake and the boat, to put down the phone he keeps reaching for in a pocket that holds nothing. He thinks he wants a better clamp: a way to mend the world's crack without spending anyone, a way to pay the debt of the Forty of Low Kiln whose warmth bought his whole return. What he needs is harder and he will spend thousands of chapters arriving at it: that repair is not restoration, that a mended vessel carries its break, that a debt can be carried instead of settled, that attention is the one thing no institution can tax, and that the answer is not a tighter seal but a third thing — a repair that leaves the crack visible and the Warm free to move.

## Bonds

**Nina Marchetti** — his daughter, the light she stopped leaving on. He loves her in the only idiom he has: practical acts, unsaid. She resents him for it. The unsent call is the shape of the debt that made him portable; the words never crossed, only their weight. He will reach for a phone for her at every important death in Sarn and never look at the name. *Ch 1:* she is the lit rectangle in the coat pocket on the expressway, the boat promised nine times, a name he will not look at; heavier than the phone.

**Deshawn Carter** — the paramedic friend who made jokes instead of asking. Their friendship was ten minutes of bad pizza and silence in a car park. Des's laugh is the sound Dan reaches for when he cannot reach Nina. The tension: Des never asked the question that would have let Dan confess, and Dan never gave him the chance. *Ch 2:* the laugh came up whole out of a car park in Chicago --- bad pizza going cold on the console, wet slush under the lamps, a gold tooth catching the light, an eleventh minute that never came, and the one minute timed on the radio after the expressway --- and went out like a lamp. There is no word in this country for a man who rode in the back of a rig and made jokes instead of asking; the ache went in under the flower and stayed.

**Lena Ostrowski** — the senior resident who taught him the first duty is to decide and the second is to carry it. He calls her the closest thing he had to a mother in the hospital and sent flowers to her retirement and did not go. Her maxim is both his gift (the courage to say *leave it*) and his excuse (the silence over Tasha's chart). She is the voice of his heresy and the voice he must finally outgrow. *Ch 2:* her correction arrived in his ear while the room was arranging him: \memory{Decide, Marchetti. Then carry it.} He carried it without deciding, which is the finer art. *Ch 3:* her full name cut across the market without a tag, in the present tense — \memory{Marchetti. You're back on in ten.} — followed by the two duties and the trouble with the second. For one breath the pigs and the trauma bay shared an air: ceiling light, disinfectant, a girl of twenty-two on a table, a resident waiting for the sentence. He reached for a pocket with no phone. He understood that in the bay he had decided and not stayed, and that here he was neither the man who decides nor the man who carries but the thing carried, the duties performed on him by other people.

**Paul Vesci** — the rival who called him the butcher. Vesci is the man Dan could become: a man whose institution absorbs his guilt and hands him back clean. Dan reported him once and has spent fifteen years being called a butcher for it; the secret of Tasha's chart makes the nickname true in a way Vesci cannot know. They pull against each other across a gulf of mutual, unspoken recognition. *Ch 2:* he heard his own life rewritten behind him as a chart that said one thing and a room that had done another, with a steady hand and a signature at the bottom to make the two agree --- the grammar he learned in Vesci's hospital and has never unlearned. *Ch 3:* \memory{That's what a chart is for.} went outward for the first time, out of Berr Clay's mouth in a market, in the sentence *that is a kindness, and kindness is entered*. Dan had kept the grammar inside his head for two days; now it had a voice and a green ring at a man's mouth, and was no less reasonable for being in the open.

**Marra** — the collector in the grey coat at the crash, who wrote his measure and paid for his return with her own fingers. He does not know her name in the early chapters; she is the woman at the edge of the flare light. She has been paying for him ever since. *Ch 1:* he has filed her as a coat, a head, a hand, and the rhythm of a pause between strokes; he has not put the hand at the flare beside the dark hand on the birth-slate, though both are on the page. *Ch 2:* he set the pause in the neat dark hand on the birth-slate beside the pause in the writing hand at the flare a second time, and did not join them. *Ch 3:* he saw the same stop a third time, on the upper line of a slate at the Court's table at the Cross, in a strange neat hand that set the name down before anybody looked at the face. The clerk is not she and has not been joined to her; the three pauses lie side by side and no fourth is made.

**Sella Vail** — the widow who took him in and has never once asked where his memory came from. She is waiting to see what he will become. Her silence is its own kind of Court: kindness that does not want an answer. *Ch 1:* her hand came in ahead of her, felt for the heat at his belly, found none, opened and shut once over the bundle and withdrew; she could name the house that kept the fire but not the hand in the door. *Ch 2:* the hovering hand became a taking hand, measured and unhurried and knowing what the thing would bear; she said *the boy* and *the house*, laid him on fresh straw, told the pot not to be set near the door, and wrote him down as nothing he could see. *Ch 3:* she carried him into the Cross in a basket under a fold of wool, carried him like a pot filled that must not be slopped, and kept a half-pace from every open slate. She put the fold back without looking and said *the house brings him* to a woman who asked whose house; she answered the question about the mother with *Account-day*. She did not answer Berr about the widow's mark and said nothing on the walk home about where they had been or what had been read over him.

**Alda Reed** — the midwife who delivered him, reached for the slate before the child, crossed two fingers over the flower and called it a birth-flower. *Ch 1:* he read her order of reaching, her tin touched twice, and her exact dates covering a thing she would not say. *Ch 2:* she washed him in the same order, reached for the slate first, touched the tin, crossed the sign at the corner of the flower in front of him without knowing she had done it, and told Dessa he had gone in the levy. *Ch 3:* at the edge of the market she kept the birth-slates, touched the tin a third time, saw the red edge of the flower at his wrist where the wool had ridden back, made the two-finger sign without knowing she did it in front of a market full of people, and covered it with a date nobody had asked for --- *Stone seven; the account went up on the eighth, before the paper went out.*

**Dessa Silt** — the mother who will not name him. *Ch 1:* he read the pulse that rose when her count stopped, the sieve-wire scar she rubbed, and the two fingers that stayed too long pressing the wool flat at his shoulder. *Ch 2:* she followed the bundle to the end of the yard, counted nineteen steps she did not take, stood with her pulse in her throat and her thumb on the pale scar and the door-shut smile on her face, and let him go believing he had been spent among the levy children.

**Mira Silt** — the aunt who knows. *Ch 1:* she stood in the one patch of floor the slate did not reach, said the secret was in what happens to a child after it is born, and crossed the room to give bread without once looking at the writing. *Ch 2:* she is not on stage; the brown-edged crust from her hand is still the last warmth that crossed House Silt before the boy was taken. *Ch 3:* a background figure at the eel stall in the afternoon, eel slime dried on her sleeves and the top of her left ear gone; she asked Berr the eel price and answered it herself, shoved a loose board clear with the side of her hand, and never once looked at the basket. He put her not-looking beside the other kind --- the man on the block priced him, this woman kept him --- and had no word for either.

**Jorra Vail** — the son of the house, sixteen and old in the hands, who said *he's not Vail* before the boy came under the roof and was refused an answer. *Ch 2:* the arrival sealed the sentence --- a child under the roof and nobody wrote him as Vail, which was what Jorra wanted and what he could not bear. He did not look at Sella, obeyed her before she had finished, spoke to the wheel and the pots and once to Tarn, and never addressed the boy.

**Tarn** — the house's clay-bound hand, six years into a seven-year term. *Ch 2:* he was given the loft above the wheel-shed while the boy was given the bed, and said *He's a foundling. I'm a debt. Don't put us in the same sentence, Sella.* He took the small bench knife out and put it back where it could be checked, banked the coals, and stopped his broom for two strokes at the flat note of a pot set down, without saying what the sound was.

**Garl Vail** — the husband of the house, dead in the Long Cold and never collected. *Ch 2:* Dan read him not as a man but as a space --- a seat worn hollow on the left, a salt-stiff coat on its peg, an Ash ridge the width of two thumbs, a proverb in the house's mouth --- and counted him four, four and a gap. *Ch 3:* he is present as the space between Berr and Sella --- the uncalled debt against a firing of tiles, spoken around and never named, left where it lay as carefully as the Ash ridge --- and as the grey load going out in a barrow toward the north gate, which Dan put with the ridge and the charred slate and did not ask to have explained.

**Berr Clay, the Thane of Clay** — new in this chapter and already the local face of the Court. He read the figure over the top of the basket twice, wiped his thumb on his apron before touching the slate both times, and never said *I*; he confirmed Sella's widow's mark as a kindness that is entered, left Garl's debt uncalled, and went back to the moot with the red cord folded square. Dan recognized him perfectly, which was the worst of it: a man with a clean slate and a good reason, gone home thinking he had done the district a kindness. Berr watched him with a kiln-master's eye and did not look into the basket.

**Ulda** — a small foundling in somebody else's cast-offs who comes into the yard in this chapter and is not named in the narration: a gap in his grin, a green sliver of lead-glaze on a string at his throat, an affectionate voice that finishes another person's sentence for them. *Ch 16:* he finished a kiln-hand's sentence at the well, turned his head toward a pot a half-beat after the boy's and could not say why he had turned, rubbed the green sliver, and took a warm loaf from a man from Slate Row with no slate between them. The boy set the delay, the loaf and the unfinished sentence on separate places and wrote no cause across any of them. He is a shape in the private file, not yet a betrayal paid, and the loaf has begun to gather interest in an account he cannot read.

**Ch 4.** Sella carried him to the Drawing and back without a word or a look into the basket, touched the mark before the kiln and not at the bank, and put the fold right without a look; the wall held. Alda reached for the slate first, touched the tin twice, crossed the sign at his wrist and again at another child's hairline, and hid two notes neither of which he saw. Tarn's broom stopped at the Vail pot's note and he said nothing --- the second ear, seen only by the reader --- and the boy kept the interval beside his own; two people keeping the same sound without a word between them was the nearest thing to an equality the house had. Jorra spoke to the pots, shifted the damper twice, and came in from the back room with slate dust on his forearms saying *the back room's cold*; the boy wanted to know which slate had been broken. Dessa stood at her threshold counting nineteen into her thumb and did not look into the basket; she has made the figure at the Cross into a person laid out of reach. Garl's coals held all night, his seat stayed hollow and his proverb went to the pot while the uncalled debt lay beside a measure that was called. The collector at the Drawing had grey fingertips and a reason and was neither Marra nor the Cross clerk; his neat hand had no pause in it, and the boy left the three pauses where they lay.

**Ch 5.** Two ears under one roof became two silences kept side by side: on Stone 13 he turned his head toward a pot *before* it was set down, and Tarn, come up the yard with the knife already back on its sill, stopped half a stroke at the soft set-down that gave nothing and said nothing about either. The pot's absent note became an interval kept as an object; the hearing count stayed five from four sources. Sella fed him from the house's own dish and gave every instruction to the fire, carried him down twice and to the market edge once, kept her half-pace, left the crust where it was put and said nothing of what she had seen. Jorra's forearms held slip and no slate dust, and *he's not Vail* stayed sealed. Mira answered *warm* off the slate, touched her grey cord when a figure was spoken, and put a crust briskly into the fold; Dessa, at the end of nineteen steps, counted him among the bits and never looked in. A green-mouthed silhouette across the market was filed without being named. A collector's soft cuff crossing the bridge brought back a cafeteria at four in the morning and a wrapper eaten to the last crumb --- hunger and thrift the same appetite. Low Kiln still said three days to the Five and would not say what it counted.

**Ch 6.** The woman in the grey coat's pause came a fourth time, at the Court's table on Stone 20, in a slate whose upper line was the neat dark hand; he set it beside the flare, the birth-slate and the Cross of Stone 10 and it did not join them, while the Drawing collector's hand with no pause stayed in its own place. Sella carried him down to the Cross and back at a half-pace from every open slate, touched the mark only before openings, slid his basket out of the draught without looking at his face and left the cold crust where Mira had put it. Alda came under the roof, reached for the slate first, crossed the sign at his wrist unaware and covered it with a date, wrote a second note turned face-down toward a floorboard, and said *warm and breathing* exactly, which was not true. Tarn went still for two breaths at *you've done well*, held the pale clip-ring to the light, announced the knife twice and stopped his broom at the empty set-down; two ears kept their silence under one roof. Jorra worked five days in bone-coloured slip with no dust and moved the damper before she had finished. Berr read the figure again and went around Garl's debt without looking into the basket. Garl stayed a coat, a hollow seat, an unswept ridge and a proverb, spoken around at the Cross for another year.

**Ch 7.** Sella counted fuel and bread into the last tend in loaves, glances and the space where a loaf should have stood, and touched the widow\'s mark only before an opening; on Stone 30 she carried him down to the last market and set him in the moot-post\'s shade and put the fold right without looking at his face, and said nothing about the two crusts in the fold. Tarn\'s head turned a degree when a flat note went past the door on a vessel somebody carried, and his broom checked at the place where a pot cracked in the yard; two ears kept the same silence. Jorra\'s dust came back on Stone 17 after five clean mornings, and he knew the full note of a Vail pot before it left the wheel but would not say so in front of a kiln-master. Alda touched the tin unopened, made the sign at his wrist unaware and covered it with a date, and wrote a further note turned face-down toward the floorboard. Mira put a fresh crust beside the spent one, called it a crust and not a sermon, and touched her grey cord when a figure was spoken; Dessa\'s cough went on being weather. Berr read one in twelve for the feast and went around Garl\'s debt as Sella went around Ash; Garl stayed the coat, the hollow seat, the ridge measured by a thumb against the death-house ridge and a proverb said to the pot. The pause in the neat dark hand came a fifth time at the market\'s edge and lay with the four without joining them.

**Ch 8.** Through the Five he watched a fire come into the house from a source nobody named and extended his count to the light it made --- four, four, and a place where a fifth would be if there were a fifth. Sella touched the widow's mark before the opening and at no other hour; the year's first measure was taken offstage and entered without a figure in the house. At the Weighing a clean voice in a temple's clothes read a whole return for somebody absent and a name the household would not repeat, and he filed the silence after it among the gaps. The Court's room reached him only as a voice through a doorway --- bench, pot, draught and a missing line --- and he set it beside the birth room, the kiln-house and the death-house, writing no cause. In the Long Night the house told the Return in the dark and the bell came back four, four and a gap in the telling, which he kept beside the heard bell in two places; cold came at cheek, temple and wrist and neither the breast nor the crust was given. Under the Vail roof Garl's name was not spoken and Alda laid dates where a name should stand, crossing two fingers at the birth-flower without knowing it; the hand went to the pocket-place and found wool where a lit rectangle with a name had lain, and the boat promise came on cold water in past tense, untagged. On the Last Day no third crust came into the fold; Mira answered a question with a question and her own answer, touched the grey cord under her apron and showed the second cord twisted twice, the knots uncounted. Sella would not let the Ash be swept early, measured both ridges with one thumb and left them where they lay, then burned the grey in the morning and called it dealt with. On Ilmen 1 the coals were still banked, so the mark stayed untouched; the slates were opened in Slate Row spoken of as a direction, one in twelve printed and unchallenged, and he carried his count into a new year the district had already entered. Nothing joined, nothing explained; his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the warmth and came down.

**Ch 9.** Through Days 31–37 he kept the count exactly while the household kept its number badly in fists, mouths and weather. On Ilmen 2 the well water tasted different and a man called the ice honeyed under the mud, and nobody said the word for the thaw; on Ilmen 4 Jorra's grey dust came back from the back room on forearms the boy filed apart from clean slip; on Ilmen 5 Alda laid a date where a name should stand, touched the unopened tin and wrote a fourth hidden note turned face-down toward a floorboard; on Ilmen 6 a pot answered nearly full and he filed it as the first earthly answer to the Stone 13 interval, advanced and not paid; on Ilmen 7 Tarn's head went a degree toward that sound and the Singing Sill gave its note early; on Ilmen 8 Dessa's cough was worse and still weather, her thumb went over the sieve-wire scar, and the shape of her hand on the basket handle reached in past tense, untagged, toward a pocket-place and a lit rectangle with a name he did not look at. He wanted the smoky crust and could not reach it. Sella carried him up the yard at dusk with two crusts in the fold and no third, and his hand went up an inch toward the warmth and came down.

**Ch 10.** Dessa Silt is taken by the clay-heavy rise on Day 38 and dies uncollected; he is held on the bank and cannot move, speak or look away, and the small flat knock out of her is the sixth hearing of the full note, the second from a person, which goes beside the five from four sources and never among them. The detail held since Ilmen 8 is paid in past tense, untagged: a hand in a grey sleeve going into a coat pocket for a small cold rectangle with a name unlooked-at, a boat nine promises old on cold water, the hand coming back with wool, nothing crossing into the basket. Alda comes after the vessel has given and lays dates where a wonder should stand; Mira keeps her two cords side by side and brings no crust; on Day 44 Orra Weir --- newly known to him by silt, tallow and rope-hands --- ties a forty-first knot she cannot account for, says *forty* low into the basket and watches what he does with it, and he cannot answer. Sella carries him home at dusk, sets him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, and puts the fold right without looking at his face.

**Ch 11.** Sella carried him down under the north bridge at dawn and set him out of the draught, then up the Clay Yard at dusk and onto his bed; every instruction went to the fire, the pot and the wool, and she did not look into the basket. Tarn's knife went back to its sill and was announced to the empty yard on the first two mornings, and on the third he told nobody because the yard had already been told; six years counted, the seventh held back, his broom over the collector's road without turning. Jorra's bone-coloured forearms went in ahead of his hand at the damper and he told a pot it would do without a look toward the bed. Alda came short with the birth-slates, touched the tin once unopened, crossed the sign at the flower unaware and covered it with a date, saying *warm and breathing*, exact and not true. Berr came up the yard with a figure he would not give and took himself out of his own sentence. Mira's two cords and Orra's knot-string stood on separate boards; Orra watched him watching the readable shape of the forty-first knot and did not say the word again.

**Ch 12.** The old life stayed offstage again --- no present-tense break and no tagged memory --- while the surgeon's register read the slack oiled paper, the stopped barley breath and the empty place in the fold as exactly as it once read a chart. Nina's lit name was not reached for; Des, Ostrowski and Vesci returned only as the district's grammar, in Berr's passive sentences that hand a figure to a slate and take the speaker out of the room, and in the order of reaching --- slate before child, date before wonder --- that stood in for all of them. Sella's hand, Alda's hand and Mira's hand stayed on the tray and did not become a cause; Orra's watching was only a board beside Mira's, the knot's shape legible and the word unspoken. Mira's crust had gone quietly out of the fold on Day 46 and nobody said it was gone.

**Ch 13.** Sella carried him three doors down to House Silt and set the basket just inside a finger's-width-open door, away from every open slate, then carried him home at a half-pace and set him on his bed under the roof; she still said *the house* where another woman would say *I*, and had still not said who paid for any warmth. Alda came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and wrote no sixth note this chapter; Mira at the last market answered a question with her own answer, put her thumb to the grey cord with a second twisted twice beneath it, and brought no crust; Berr wiped his thumb twice, read one in twelve twice over the basket and took himself out of his own sentence. Tarn agreed with the early thaw and the late and kept wanting the late one; Jorra's bone-coloured forearms went in ahead of his hand at the damper. Garl stayed a coat, a hollow seat, a clean dark line and an uncalled debt; Dessa stayed dead twenty-two mornings with her tin above the bed unopened. A stranger at the well described, as history eighteen leagues east, the very danger under his own roof --- a counting room shut in the war, the damper closed, men dead of the breath --- and nobody joined it to the kiln. He filed it on a tray of its own and wrote no cause across it.

**Ch 14.** Through Days 61–67 he is sixty-one, then sixty-seven days old, and the count buys him a fifth low thing he cannot spend. Under the Vail roof and at the rack's edge he hears water going over water behind the drying racks and sets it on a place of its own, not a hearing and not a vessel's note, beside four low sounds in four places that stay four. Sella's instruction still goes to the fire, the wool and the bucket and never to the basket; she says *the house*, keeps her half-pace and touches the widow's mark at no hour in seven days, because a rack, a well, a door and a bed are not openings. Tarn announces his knife, counts six years and holds the seventh nearly gone, checks his broom at the cracked-pot place and turns his head a degree toward a nearly-full pot before going back to sweeping. Jorra brings grey slate dust out of the room he cannot enter, moves the damper ahead of Sella's sentence and keeps *he's not Vail* sealed. In House Silt on Silt 5 Alda writes the sixth page in the second hand and turns it toward a floorboard, while the pale stone is gone from the fold and the pewter tin above the bed keeps its thumb-bright lid shut; he can enter neither. Mira touches her grey cord when a figure is spoken and shows the second twisted twice beneath it; Berr wipes his thumb twice and takes himself out of his own sentence. On the last day the ice is honeyed all through, the current shows in veins, and one crust lies stiff in the fold with the empty place beside it unnamed. Nothing is joined, paid, explained or ended.

**Ch 15 — the flood and what it left.** Sella carried him down to the edge of the brown sheet and up again at the half-pace for seven days, gave every instruction to the fire, the wool and the bucket and never to the basket, said *the house*, stood over the clean dark line and the new finer grey with a broom and swept neither, told the fire it was the thaw, and left the thread at his mouth frozen and unwiped at the close. Tarn kept the small knife on its sill with six years counted and the seventh nearly gone, his broom over the collector's road without turning and his head not turning at a vessel's note past the door on Silt 15. Jorra brought grey slate dust out of the back room on Silt 14 with two men unnamed, moved the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, came through on Silt 15 with bone-coloured slip and no grey worked past the bent finger, and kept *he's not Vail* sealed. Mira worked the eels as the water fell, touched the grey cord when a buyer named a figure, showed the second cord twisted twice beneath it with neither knot counted, and kept her board beside Orra's without touching it; Orra stood at the flood's edge with her board and cord and got no answer. Berr read one in twelve over the standing water on Account-day, wiped his thumb twice, took himself out of his own sentence and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked. Alda's sixth page and the pewter tin above the empty bed stayed shut three doors down; Garl was kept by his salt-stiff coat, hollow seat, clean dark line and proverb said to a pot with no crack; Dessa stayed dead and uncollected, her name and the number in her thumb unsaid.

**Ch 16.** Through Days 75–77 he is seventy-five, then seventy-six, then seventy-seven days old, and the water goes out of the yard leaving mud that takes a boot and gives it back slowly. Recovery shows itself as another kind of keeping-by-avoidance: a yard that sweeps around the clean dark line, the finer grey and the space under the lowest rack with a broom and a glance and no figure spoken; a house that gives every instruction to the fire, the wool, the bucket and the broom and never to the basket; a woman who says *the house* and goes wide around every open slate. He keeps seventy-five, seventy-six, seventy-seven exactly against the household's arithmetic in loaves, glances and a space at the row's end nobody calls a number, while the figure for the flood goes from fist to mouth to shoulder and arrives nowhere. At the well a small foundling in cast-off clothes finishes a kiln-hand's sentence in an affectionate voice and makes the yard laugh; when a pot is set down the boy's head goes toward it before the knuckle and the child's goes a half-beat after, and the child rubs the green sliver at his neck and cannot say why he turned. A man from Slate Row whose name is on no roll gives the child a warm loaf with no slate between them, and the boy keeps the delay and the loaf and the unfinished sentence on separate places and writes no cause across any of them, because a better-lit room taught him what three likenesses in a row will do to a man who wants an answer. He does not call it a sale. At the Fishstairs the steps have come back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that nobody makes a fall of, and the soft-cuffed clerk still sits for a measure nobody called. Mira answers a question with her own answer, shows the second cord twisted twice beneath the grey with neither knot counted, and keeps her board beside Orra's without touching it. Three doors down Alda lays her birth-slates down before turning to the fold, crosses two fingers at the flower unknowing, covers the sign with a date given a woman thirty-nine mornings dead, says *warm and breathing* which is exact and not true, and turns her sixth slate toward a floorboard. Jorra comes back with grey slate dust past his little finger and moves the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence; Tarn's seventh year is nearly gone and still held, his broom goes over the collector's road without turning and his head does not turn at a whole vessel's note. Six hearings stay six, three near-full answers stay advanced and not paid, and the word forty is not spoken. One crust lies stiff in the fold with the empty place beside it unnamed and no third comes. At dusk Sella sets him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, puts the fold right without looking at his face and leaves the thread at his mouth frozen and unwiped; the door stands a finger's width open with the river's note through the gap and the fire's four, four and a gap through the floor kept apart. The count crosses into Silt 19 unspent. No experiment, no rank change, no recognition, no ending.

**Ch 17.** Through Silt 19–25 he is carried at the edge of the work while the lean week counts what the water took and changes nothing. Sella's instruction still goes to the fire, the wool, the bucket and the broom and never to the basket; she says *the house*, keeps her half-pace, sets the basket at the edge of the light three doors down and leaves the widow's mark untouched, because a well and a reading and a bed are not openings. Tarn's knife is on its sill with six counted and the seventh nearly gone and still held, and he tells the kindling *Six counted. The next I don't count*; his broom goes over the collector's road without turning and his head does not turn at the note past the door. Jorra's grey dust comes back out of the room behind the finger's-width-open door with the men unnamed, and his bent finger moves the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence; *he's not Vail* stays sealed. Alda lays her slates writing-down before the fold, crosses the sign unknowing, covers it with the date she gave a woman now forty mornings dead and says *warm and breathing*, exact and not true, with the tin in her bodice and her sixth page both shut. Mira answers a question with her own answer at the eel boards, touches the grey cord when a figure is named and shows the second twisted twice beneath it with neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's without touching. Berr reads one in twelve over a thinner crowd, wipes his thumb twice, takes himself out of his own sentence and goes off believing he has done a kindness, the box still locked. Ulda is glimpsed once, silently, at the well, a half-beat and a green sliver and no slate passed. No new scar, possession or change of voice: the old life stays offstage and nothing is spent.

**Ch 20 (bonds).** Sella still carries him at the half-pace and has not said whose child he is; Alda's sign, exact date and shut tin stand as they stood; Mira's second cord and Orra's board are still unread and not touching; Jorra's *he's not Vail* and his promise to the men who burn slates are unchanged; Tarn's seventh year is nearly gone and still held and his ear is still kept silent; Berr's box is locked and his good reason intact; Ulda remains a shape in the yard, not yet a betrayer and not yet a speaker. No bond has changed hands this chapter: no name given, no secret sold, no tin or box opened.

**Ch 21 (relationships at the close).** On Seed 12 a neighbour at the loaf shelf reached past the bare board and named the row's old space as a thing the planting brought in and the empty place at his ear as a child's appetite in one breath, her hand going to the board before her eyes went to him; he filed the two tellings on two places and did not make her joining his own (T097 planted). Sella still said *the house*, gave every instruction to the pot, the fire, the broom and the wool and never to the basket, carried him at the half-pace from every open slate and set him on his bed under the roof each dusk without looking at his face; she heard the neighbour's two sayings and did not join them either, and her private roll stayed unshown. Tarn kept six counted and the seventh nearly gone and held, told the kindling *Six counted. The next I don't count*, swept the collector's road without turning and kept the unforged sentence with the seventh year, entering neither. Jorra came through on Seed 13 with grey slate dust past the bent finger from men he did not name, put that finger into the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and read the iron as the bite that holds and takes with no Grathic word for both; *he's not Vail* stayed sealed and the promise of the name was neither given nor withdrawn. Berr came up the clay road with the cord folded under his coat, wiped his thumb twice, read one in twelve, called the levy and took himself out of his own sentence with *There'll be a slate at the watch*, going off toward High Kiln with the box locked and believing he had done a kindness. Mira worked the eels on Seed 14 and showed the second cord twisted twice beneath the grey with neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's and not touching; she did not look into the basket. Alda laid her birth-slates before the fold three doors down, crossed the flower unknowing, covered it with the date she gave Dessa, said *Warm and breathing* exact and not true and touched the tin in her bodice without opening it; her sixth page stayed face-down and unread and the pewter tin above the empty bed stayed shut. No bond changed hands, no tin or box was opened, and no name, number or seventh hearing came.

**Ch 22 (relationships at the close).** On Seed 15 he was carried to the worked strips east of Low Kiln at one hundred and four days old and watched the hands the strips had been said to want go in, seed out of fists in handfuls nobody counted; a woman closed her fist on nothing with chaff in its creases and would not say what it held, and a man with a barrow opened his mouth on the number of hands and thought better of it, so that the district's withholding was performed inside the planting itself. He set the direction he had carried for a month beside the number the yard would not give and did not join them; his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the worked strip and came down. Sella gave the instruction to the bucket and the broom before anybody and never to the basket, said *the house*, kept her half-pace from every open slate and went around the space under the lowest rack, and told the fire it was the thaw; the fire answered four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed. At the loaf shelf a neighbour said the old place at the row's end had come in with the planting and went off with her pot, and he filed the saying beside the one T097 waits on without making her joining his own, keeping the shelf's two gaps in the other room from touching the fold's one crust and its unnamed empty place. On Seed 16 men unnamed came before first grey into the finger's-width-open back room; Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust worked past the bent little finger, spoke to the wheel and a pot, put that finger into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and read the iron as the bite that holds and takes with no Grathic word for both, joined to no kiln. At the well Tarn agreed with the early thaw and the late, wanted the late because it puts off the paying, his chest tight since the dark, his knife on its sill with six counted and the seventh nearly gone and still held, *Six counted. The next I don't count*, the unforged collector sentence kept with it, his broom over the road without turning. On Seed 17 Sella set the basket at the end of the fish-trade shade, instruction to wool and water and never to the basket; Mira answered a question with her own answer, moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie, touched the grey cord when a buyer named a figure and showed the second twisted twice beneath it with neither knot counted and her board beside Orra's not touching; the soft-cuffed clerk still sat for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, with no sixth sighting. Midday three doors down he watched Alda lay her birth-slates before the fold, press the wool too long, cross the flower unknowing, cover it with the date she gave Dessa, say *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down* exact and not true and touch her tin unopened, her sixth page still face-down and six pages unread. Under the roof he found the file unchanged: six hearings beside and never among with no seventh, three near-full answers advanced and not paid, three intervals, four low things in four places with the river's note a fifth, the two tellings of the shut room joined to no kiln, the word forty unspoken, the damper's double bite filed as an object and the loan uncalled; no collector came for the woman in the brook and the morning came all the same, one crust stiff in the fold and two unnamed gaps on the shelf in a room that does not touch it. Sella told the fire it was the thaw, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot and did not look toward the bed; his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the note of water over water and came down. The old life stayed offstage: the surgeon's register read a fist of seed as the ninety-second sorting and the damper as a clamp and a tourniquet in one piece of iron, exact, complete and worth nothing. No new scar, possession, knowledge or change of voice; the count stands at one hundred and six, unspent.

**Ch 23 (relationships at the close).** On Seed 18–20 nothing was joined, paid or spoken across three lived days. Sella still carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave every instruction to the broom, bucket, wool, water, pot and fire and never to the basket, said *the house*, set him on his bed under the roof each dusk and left the thread frozen; she pushed a strand of coal-black hair back with the wrist that carries the burn, stood a moment at the fire counting something under her breath toward no opening, and her grey streak at the temple showed pale. Tarn's seventh year lay at the very edge of the sill with the breath still unspent and the unforged collector sentence beside it; he said *Six counted. The next I don't count* and *I owe six*, swept the collector's road without turning, and took the broom only after she had named it. Jorra gave the men in the back room no name, put his bent finger into the damper ahead of her sentence and read the double bite as an object joined to no kiln. Alda laid her slates down before the fold, crossed the flower unknowing, covered it with the date she gave Dessa, said *Warm and breathing* exact and not true, and kept her sixth page and both tins shut. Mira answered a question about the run with her own answer, touched the grey cord when a figure was named and showed the second twisted twice beneath it, her board beside Orra's and not touching; Orra watched from where she watched and got no answer. Berr read one in twelve over the worked strips, took himself out of his own sentence and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked, believing he had done a kindness. Ulda was glimpsed unnamed and silent at the market's edge, his head half a beat late toward a pot, the green sliver rubbed, no loaf, no slate, no sale. No bond changed hands: no name given, no knot counted, no tin or box opened, no seventh hearing, no sale or betrayal paid.

**Ch 24 (relationships at the close).** Sella still carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave every instruction to the bucket, broom, wool, water, pot and fire and never to the basket, said *the house*, moved her lips along a loaf row standing with two gaps and glanced at the clay jar without giving a figure, told the fire it was the thaw and *The house will keep it*, and stood a moment with the back of the broken pot in her hand counting under her breath toward no opening while the grey streak at her temple showed pale; she heard the neighbour's one-breath joining and did not join it to the empty place at his ear, and the private roll stayed unshown. Tarn's seventh year stood at the very edge of the sill with the breath from Seed 16 still unspent, and for the first time his broom checked half a beat behind Jorra's finger in the damper before he went on without a word or a turn of the head; neither man named the other's silence and the loan stayed uncalled. Jorra gave the men in the back room no name, came through with grey slate dust past the bent finger, spoke to the wheel and a pot and put that finger into the damper ahead of Sella's sentence; *he's not Vail* stayed sealed and the promise of the name to the slate-burners was neither given nor withdrawn. Berr went off up the clay road with the narrow slate under his coat and the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness, taking himself out of his own sentence. Mira's thumb went to the grey cord and showed the second twist with neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's not touching; Alda covered the flower with a dead woman's date, said *Warm and breathing* exact and not true and kept her tin and sixth page shut; Dessa stayed dead and unnamed and Ulda was offstage. No bond changed hands: no name given, no knot counted, no tin or box opened, no sale or betrayal paid and no seventh hearing.

**Ch 25 (relationships at the close).** Sella still carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave every instruction to the bucket, the broom, the pot, the wool, the water and the fire and never to the basket, said *the house*, moved her lips along a loaf row standing with two gaps, sent a woman to the pot with *the house has said it*, told the fire it was the thaw and said *The house will keep it*, stood a moment counting under her breath with the grey streak pale at her temple and did not look toward the bed; she heard the neighbour's one-breath joining and did not join it to the empty place at his ear. Tarn took the broom only after its instruction and on Seed 25 his stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check it had made the day before, the stones keeping the place where it had stopped, chest tight, the breath from Seed 16 unspent, the knife at the very edge of its sill and the loan uncalled. Jorra gave the men in the back room no name, came through with grey slate dust past the bent finger, spoke to the wheel and a pot and put that finger into the damper ahead of her unfinished sentence; *he's not Vail* stayed sealed and the promise to the slate-burners was neither given nor withdrawn. Alda laid the date over the flower and said *Warm and breathing* exact and not true with her sixth page and both tins shut; Mira's second cord and Orra's board stayed unread and not touching; Berr's twice-wiped thumb, the soft hands at the Cross and the clerk's uncalled measure stayed on separate trays. Dessa stayed dead and unnamed to him; Ulda was offstage. No bond changed hands: no name given, no knot counted, no tin or box opened, no sale or betrayal paid and no seventh hearing.

**Ch 26 (bonds).** Nothing changed hands across Seed 27–30. Sella still carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave every instruction to the bucket, broom, pot, fire, wool and water and never to the basket, said *the house*, set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without a look at his face and left the thread frozen; the private roll stayed unshown and his number was spoken by nobody. With Tarn the equality held at the place it had moved to: the seventh year pressed to the very edge of the sill, the breath from Seed 16 unspent, the stroke going on over the flagstones without the half-check while the stones kept the stopped place, neither man naming the other's silence and the loan still uncalled. With Jorra the grey dust from men unnamed, the bent finger in the cold damper ahead of Sella's sentence, *he's not Vail* sealed and the promise to the slate-burners ungiven; he did not look toward the bed or the basket. With Alda, Mira and Berr their records and hands stayed on their own trays: six pages and three tins shut, two cords twisted twice with neither knot counted, the twice-wiped thumb and the finger over a figure that did not know the missing hand. No name given, no knot counted, no tin or box opened, no sale, no seventh hearing and no ending.

**Ch 27 (bonds).** Nothing changed hands across Reed 1–3. Sella still carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave the bucket, broom, pot and fire their instructions before anybody and never the basket, said *the house*, went around the space beneath the lowest rack and the missing hand's place as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it, and at the going of the light set him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen; her hand rested a half-beat longer than the setting needed and the grey streak showed pale. With Tarn the equality held at the place it had moved to: the seventh year pressed past the end of the planting and still held, the knife at the edge of its sill, the breath from Seed 16 unspent, and the unchecking stroke kept beside the earlier half-beat with no cause across them; the loan was not called. With Jorra the men in the back room were given no name, grey slate dust was ground past the bent finger, the damper's double bite was read as an object joined to no kiln, and *he's not Vail* and the promise to the slate-burners stayed sealed. Mira's not-looking was a keeping beside Orra's board and knot-string, six pages and two tins stayed shut, Berr's twice-wiped thumb stayed on its tray, and the foundling's fifth half-beat at the market was not a sale. No name given, no knot counted, no tin opened, no seventh hearing and no ending.

**Ch 28 (bonds).** Nothing changed hands across Reed 4–6, and the one thing that moved did so without a hand on it. Under the Vail roof the sentence that would call Tarn's loan came all the way to the threshold of the word for the first time and stopped there — an interval on the tray, not a payment, with no figure and no name — and he filed it beside the half-beat, the unchecking stroke and his own held breath from the market, writing no cause across any of them. Sella still carried him at the half-pace, gave the bucket, broom, pot, fire, wool and wind their instructions and never the basket, said *the house*, went around the rack space and the missing hand's place as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it, left the thread frozen and her hand resting a half-beat longer than the setting needed, and sent a woman asking the reeds' cost to the pot. Tarn's seventh year was pressed past the third tend and still held; Jorra's bent finger stood in the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence while his stroke went on without the half-check, the two silences unjoined and *he's not Vail* sealed. At the Weir Mira's second twist showed beneath the grey with neither knot counted and her board beside Orra's not touching; Orra gave a date where a levy figure was asked, and the reed-cutter's tally went back inside his coat unread, a third private record touching none of them. Berr read one in twelve from the clay road and took himself out of his own sentence with *your house*, never *I*. No name, no figure, no knot, no tin, no box, no seventh hearing and no sale.

**Ch 29 (bonds).** Nothing changed hands across Reed 7–9, and the one thing that moved did so a second time without a hand on it. Under the Vail roof the sentence that would call Tarn's loan came again all the way to the word that would call it and stopped there a second time, with no figure and no name; he filed it beside the first on the interval tray as a second interval and not a second payment, and the loan stayed uncalled while the seventh year was pressed further past its third tend and still held, the knife at the edge of its sill, the breath from Seed 16 unspent. Sella carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave the instruction to the wool and the river wind, the bucket, the broom, the pot and the fire and never to the basket, said *the house*, went around the clean dark line, the thaw, the rack space and the place where a hand went in and did not come back as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it, set him on his bed under the roof each dusk and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without a look at his face, left the thread frozen, her hand resting a half-beat longer than the setting needed; at the yard she sent a woman asking the reeds' cost to the pot with *the house has said it*, and at the Cross she set the basket in the moot-post's shade and kept a half-pace from every slate. With Jorra men unnamed came before first grey, fine grey slate dust was ground small as meal past the bent finger, the full note of a pot was known before a High Kiln kiln-master and withheld, and the bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of an unfinished sentence; *he's not Vail* stayed sealed and the promise of the name neither given nor withdrawn. At the market a third green sat at the corner of a silent woman's mouth, with a pale line on the back of her hand where something had once been worn tight and long, and the boy held both on places of their own and joined neither to any cord, eel, door or box; three likenesses in a row wanted to become a cause and he refused the labour, which nobody in the square could see. At the Weir the reed-cutter's grey cord went back inside his coat with its knots unread, Orra gave a date where a figure was asked and the man at the stern kept his hands off the oars, Mira's second twist stayed twice beneath the grey with neither knot counted, and the soft-cuffed clerk kept his slate for a measure nobody called: four private records in materials no Court thumb would read, all visible and none enterable. Berr came up with green at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice, said a number goes into a slate and not into a bundle, read *one in twelve, as the notice says*, called the levy and denied it with *your house*, never *I*. Three doors down Alda laid her birth-slates before the fold, pressed the wool too long, crossed the flower unknowing, covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa, said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down* exact and not true, touched her tin unopened and left six pages unread. No name, no figure, no knot counted, no tin or box opened, no seventh hearing, no sixth sighting and no sale.

**Ch 30 (bonds).** On Days 129–131 nothing changed hands, and what moved moved without a hand on it. At the Cross on Reed 10 he watched Berr read *one in twelve, as the notice says* over a crowd, call the levy and take himself out of his own sentence with *your house*, never *I*, while the true subtraction stayed in the locked box up the clay road and the heard bell stood beside the told bell, four, four and a gap, nobody crossing himself. Sella carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave the instruction to the wool, the river wind, the pot, the broom and the fire and never to the basket, went around the rack space with its broken birth-slates, set him on his bed under the roof each dusk and left the thread frozen. At the low angle beneath the Vail loaf shelf he saw short charcoal marks in clusters on the UNDERSIDE of the board, pressed hard and faded nearly into the grain, a keeping put where nobody thinks to look above a row kept badly on top with its two unnamed gaps. He did not read a stroke, count a cluster, copy a mark or lay the marks beside the fold's one crust, Alda's tin, Mira's cords, Orra's knot-string, Dessa's pewter tin or the reed-cutter's cord; he set the tally on a place of its own, advanced and unpaid, held for the far-off day a hand not his lays the district's private records together (T100 planted). On Reed 11 Tarn brought the sentence about an ear worth more than the debt a **third** time to the word that would call the loan and stopped, drawing the breath he did not spend; the boy filed a third interval and not a third payment, refusing to let three stoppings stand up and call themselves a doctrine. On Reed 12 Sella put a finger beneath the board and brought it away without looking or a word; he saw the hand and made no cause of the habit. At the Weir the reed-cutter tied a knot with his teeth and put the cord back unread, Orra gave a date where a figure was asked and the man at the stern kept his hands off the oars, Mira's second twist stayed twice beneath the grey, and the soft-cuffed clerk kept his slate for a measure nobody called: private records in materials no Court thumb will read, all visible and none entered. No name, figure, knot or loaf crossed the basket; no seventh hearing, no sixth sighting and no sale.

**Ch 31 (bonds).** Through Days 132–134 nothing changed hands. Sella carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave the instruction to the wool, the river wind, the pot, the broom and the fire and never to the basket, said *the house*, set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen; twice, on Day 132 and again on Day 134, she reached a finger beneath the loaf shelf where the charcoal tally lies, without looking and without a word, and did not go back to the shelf. He did not read the marks, count them, lay them beside the two gaps above or the crust in the fold, or make a cause of the hand that goes under without looking. At the hearth on Day 133 Tarn brought the sentence about an ear worth more than the debt that holds it a **fourth** time to the word that would call the loan and stopped there with no figure and no name, drawing the breath he did not spend; Sella, within hearing, gave the end of her instruction to the pot and asked nothing, Jorra did not ask about the ear and Tarn did not ask about the room, and the two silences stayed unjoined. Jorra's grey dust came out of the back room before first grey from men he did not name, his bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of her unfinished sentence, and the full note was known before a High Kiln kiln-master and withheld; *he's not Vail* stayed sealed. At the Weir the reed-cutter's cord, Orra's date and knot-string and Mira's two cords stood as private records in materials no Court thumb could spell, all visible and none entered. At the Cross a fourth green at a silent woman's mouth was filed as a colour and not a name, five shapes counting Berr's glaze, joined to no ear; Berr bought eels two marks short, said *A number goes into a slate, not a bundle*, took himself out of his own sentence with *your house*, never *I*, and went off with the box locked. No name given, no knot counted, no sale, no seventh hearing and no ending.

**Ch 32 (bonds).** Through Days 135–137 nothing changed hands. Sella carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave the instruction to the wool and the river wind and never to the basket, reached a finger beneath the loaf shelf on Day 135 without looking, and set him on his bed at the going of the light on Day 137 with the fold righted without a look at his face; she still has not said whose child he is. Tarn brought the sentence about the ear under this roof a fifth time to the word that would call the loan and stopped there with no figure and no name, drew the breath and did not spend it, and Jorra did not ask about the ear while Tarn did not ask about the room. Jorra's grey dust came out of the back room on Day 136 and his bent finger moved the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence; Mira's second twist stayed twice beneath the grey at the Weir and the fish-trade shade with her board beside Orra's and not touching; Orra gave a date where a figure was asked and kept the forty-first double turn legible and unnamed; Berr took two pair of eels and paid two marks short with *your house*, never *I*, and his box stayed locked. Dessa stayed dead and unnamed, Alda's six pages and both tins and Dessa's pewter tin stayed shut offstage, Marra was not in Sill, Ulda and the green-mouthed woman were offstage, and no sixth sighting, no seventh hearing, no knot counted, no tin opened and no sale given.

**Ch 33 (bonds).** With Sella nothing changed hands across Reed 19–21: the half-pace from every open slate, the instruction to the wool, the river wind, the pot, the broom and the fire and never to the basket, *the house* where another woman would say *I*, the bed under the roof and not the Debt Loft, the fold put right without a look at his face, the thread left frozen, the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand. On Day 138 she reached a finger beneath the loaf shelf without looking or a word, and on Day 140 she gave no name to the men who went into the back room and did not say the room was cold; within hearing of Tarn's sixth stopping she ended her instruction at the pot and asked nothing. With Tarn the equality held at the place it had moved to --- two ears, two silences, a loan that can hear and has never been called --- and the sixth stopping cost him a breath he did not spend while his stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check. With Jorra the dust, the wheel spoken to first, the full note withheld from the High Kiln master and the bent finger in the damper ahead of her unfinished sentence went unasked about; *he's not Vail* stayed sealed and the promise of the name was neither given nor withdrawn. With Mira and Orra nothing was counted or joined: their boards lay side by side and did not touch, Mira's second twist twice beneath the grey, Orra's forty-first double turn legible and unnamed. Berr read one in twelve at the second Account-day, paid two marks short and went up the road believing he had done a kindness, the true subtraction locked in his box. The green-mouthed woman of Day 139 was the fifth at a mouth and the sixth shape of the colour, a colour and not a name, never Fenna in the narration; Alda's six pages and both tins and Dessa's pewter tin stayed shut offstage, Marra was not in Sill, Ulda and the watcher were offstage, and no sixth sighting, no seventh hearing, no knot counted, no tin or box opened and no sale came.

**Ch 34 (bonds).** Across Reed 22–24 nothing changed hands and no hand was laid across another keeping. Sella still carried him at the half-pace, gave the instruction to the wool and the river wind and never to the basket, set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face, left the thread frozen and the pot handle two inches beyond his reaching hand, and let her hand rest a half-beat longer than the setting needed; on Days 141 and 143 she reached a finger beneath the loaf shelf without a word and the charcoal stayed unread, the widow's mark untouched because a shelf and an afternoon and a dusk are not openings. A neighbour at the shelf on Day 141 named the row's old gap and the empty place at his ear under one appetite in a single breath, a further shaping of T097 held on a place of its own, advanced and not paid, with no third gap and no joining. At the hearth on Day 142 Tarn told the kindling *Six counted. The next I don't count* and brought the ear-sentence a seventh time to the word that would call the loan, stopping there with no figure and no name; the loan stayed uncalled and the seventh year stood one tend from its end. Jorra's grey dust, his wheel spoken to first, the pot's full note withheld from the High Kiln kiln-master and his bent finger in the cold damper went unasked about; *he's not Vail* stayed sealed. Berr read one in twelve at the Cross, took himself out of his own sentence with *your house*, paid two marks short and went off with the box locked. At the Weir on Day 143 Orra's readable unnamed double turn, Mira's second twist and the reed-cutter's knotted cord stood within a dozen paces of the basket, visible and none enterable, and below the sill the dark foot was still the cold to one woman and weather to the yard. No knot counted, no name given, no tin or box opened, no sale and no ending.

**Ch 35 (bonds).** Sella still carried him at the half-pace and gave every instruction to the wool, the wind, the pot, the broom and the fire and never to the basket, reached a finger beneath the loaf shelf on Day 144 without looking, and set the pot handle two inches beyond his reaching hand at dusk; she has still not said whose child he is. Tarn's eighth stopping at the word on Day 145 was brought to the threshold with no figure and no name and the loan stayed uncalled; he asked nothing within hearing, as Jorra asked nothing about the ear and Tarn nothing about the room, the two silences unjoined. Jorra came through on Day 145 with grey slate dust past the bent finger, spoke to the wheel and a pot, withheld the full note from a High Kiln kiln-master and put that finger into the cold damper ahead of her sentence; *he's not Vail* and the promise to the slate-burners stayed as they were. Mira and Orra kept their cords and knots side by side at the Weir on Days 145 and 146, neither counted, neither carried to a door; the cold foot below the sill stayed the cold to one woman and weather to the yard. Three doors down Alda crossed the flower unknowing, covered it with the date she gave Dessa and said *Warm and breathing* exact and not true over a death no living person can correct; her sixth page and both tins stayed shut. No bond changed hands: no name given, no knot counted, no tin or box opened, no loan called.

**Ch 37 (bonds).** No bond changed hands in the first three days of Tallow. Sella touched the widow's mark before the kiln mouth on Day 150 and went straight back to setting pots, and the opening made no more of him than a basket set out of the draught; Tarn's sentence came up to the threshold on Day 152 and stopped short of it, so the loan that can hear was still uncalled; Jorra's knowing of a pot's full note went to the near-full tray and not among the hearings; Mira's and Orra's boards stayed side by side and not touching; the foundling stayed offstage with his sixth glimpse gathering interest and no loaf, slate or sale. The crust two inches from his ear remained stiff, unnamed and out of reach, and nobody under any roof said how old he was.

**Ch 38 (bonds).** No bond changed hands across the seven days of Tallow 4–10. Sella still carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave the instruction to the wool, the fire, the pot and the broom and never to the basket, and brought him home from the first Account-day at the going of the light saying nothing about what had been read over him; the two rooms did not touch. Tarn took the broom only after it was instructed, and his ear-sentence did not come up at all in these seven days, so no tenth stopping stood up to join the nine and the loan stayed uncalled; Jorra's knowing of a pot's full note went to the near-full tray and not among the hearings, and neither man asked about the other's silence. At the Weir on Day 155 Mira's board lay beside Orra's and did not touch it, with the reed-cutter's cord a third record along the same boards unread. The one new shape was Hessa Rale, a slate-runner from Slate Row who carried Berr's narrow slate edge-on between the Cross and Greybank; her hand went to the slate before her eyes went to the yard, and the boy filed that order as an eighth record-first hand on a place of its own, not joined to Marra, the clerks, the cutter, the twice-wiped thumb or the finger beneath the shelf. No figure came off her slate and no private keeping was opened. The foundling stayed offstage with his sixth glimpse gathering interest, and no loaf, slate or sale was paid.

**Ch 39 (bonds).** No bond changed hands across the three lived days. Sella still carried him at the half-pace from every open slate, gave the instruction to the wool, the fire, the pot, the broom and the running water and never to the basket, sent the wet-pot neighbours’ cost to the pot without a figure, put him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, and went to her own bed in the room that does not touch his; on Day 161 she let her unfinished sentence at the damper finish instead of having Jorra’s finger finish it for her, and nobody said a word of the waiting. Jorra’s fraction is held and unspoken, not a reconciliation, not a hearing, not a payment: *he’s not Vail* stays sealed and the promise of the foundling’s name stays ungiven; Tarn’s ear-sentence went to his teeth and no further, his stroke over the flagstones without the half-check, the ninth stopping still nine with no tenth and the loan uncalled, the two silences unjoined. Downriver at the Weir Mira answered a figure with a saying and did not look into the basket; Orra gave a date where a figure was asked and the boy did not decide whether her thumb’s return was a lie or the habit of counting. Hessa Rale stayed offstage, her narrow slate unread; the foundling stayed offstage with his sixth glimpse unspent, the green never Fenna in narration, and Dessa dead and unnamed to him.

**Ch 40 (bonds).** Nothing changed hands under the Vail roof or at the Cross across seven lived days. Sella still gave every instruction to the wool, the fire, the pot, the broom and the running water and never to the basket, said *the house*, kept the half-pace, left the widow's mark where it hung at a morning, a shelf, a hearth, a dusk, a road and a square, rested her hand a half-beat longer at the setting and set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand; on Day 166 she held a finger beneath the loaf shelf for one counted breath and gave no word, and the boy kept the held beat on its own place (T100 advanced, not paid). Jorra came through on Day 164 with slate dust ground small as meal, withheld a pot's full note from a High Kiln kiln-master, no pot lost, and when Sella's voice went low his bent finger waited at the damper for her sentence to finish, the Day 161 fraction held and unspoken (T062). Tarn took the broom only after it was instructed, told the kindling *Six counted. The next I don't count*, kept nine stoppings as objects with no tenth and the loan uncalled, and said nothing of the waiting at the iron. Berr read the printed figure at the second Account-day, denied the levy with *your house*, never *I*, and went off with the box locked and the true subtraction inside; the figure read was still not the figure paid. Hessa carried his slate up the clay road edge-on with her figure unread and was joined to no other hand; Mira at the Weir and Orra with her date kept their records on boards that did not touch. No name given, no bond paid, no secret sold.

**Ch 41 (bonds).** Nothing changed hands under the Vail roof across the last working week. Sella still gave every instruction to the wool, the fire, the pot, the broom and the running water and never to the basket, said *the house*, kept her half-pace, left the widow's mark where it hung at a morning, a shelf and a dusk, set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand and let her hand rest a half-beat longer than the setting needed; on all three days her eye went once to the clay jar cold under its glaze and her hand shut half over nothing without touching it, and her finger did not go beneath the loaf shelf. Jorra came through on Day 171 with slate dust ground small as meal past the bent finger, spoke to the wheel, told a pot *You'll do*, withheld its full note from a High Kiln kiln-master who said a pot singing early generally sings itself out early, no pot lost, and held his bent finger in the cold damper until Sella's sentence finished. Tarn took the broom only after it was instructed, said *Yes, house* to an early thaw and a late, six counted where the house can check and the seventh held where it cannot, the ear-sentence to his teeth on Day 172 and no further, no tenth standing and the loan uncalled; and while Jorra's finger stood in the iron his broom checked half a beat behind it on the flagstones and went on without a word or a turn of the head.
